Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess the main thing is I’m losing a lot of time to checking things over and over. It’s not just one thing, either. Like, I’ll check my dressing supplies, or the door locks, or at work I’ll keep going back over file dimensions and revision notes, and even when I know I’ve already looked, I don’t feel like I can just move on.

It’s like there’s this feeling that if I don’t go through the whole routine properly, or enough times, something is still unfinished or unsafe or wrong. And I know that sounds excessive, but in the moment the anxiety is really uncomfortable, and it only drops after I’ve checked the same things repeatedly. So I end up spending hours on things that should take minutes, and it’s starting to get hard to manage.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s actually a big part of it. It’s not just that I decide to check something — it’s like the thought cuts in on its own and keeps coming back.

Like with cleaning a wound area, even after I’ve already done it and I can look at it and know, logically, that I cleaned it, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed one tiny spot. And then my mind jumps to, “What if that one tiny thing turns into an infection?” It feels really hard to dismiss, even if part of me knows I’m probably overestimating it.

And it’s similar at work with CAD files. I’ll review a file or dimensions or notes, and then this thought pops back in that maybe I missed some small detail, some tiny mismatch or error, and somehow that could turn into a design mistake that affects someone or hurts someone. I try to tell myself, “You already checked this, you’re being careful,” but it doesn’t really settle. It’s like reasoning with it doesn’t work the way it should. The thought just keeps coming back until I go over it again.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. It feels like I’m pushed into doing the action, not just thinking about it. The main one is rechecking. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then check them again, and then somehow I still don’t feel finished, so I go back through the same steps. Same with the door locks — I can see that it’s locked, I can even remember locking it, but I still feel like I have to physically go back and confirm it.

At work it’s a lot of reviewing file dimensions and revision notes over and over. It’s not enough to just look once and say, “Okay, that’s done.” I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. And that can take a long time. Things that should be quick end up taking hours because I keep repeating the same checking actions until it feels… I don’t know, safe enough or complete enough.

  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that’s pretty much what it feels like. It’s not just checking for the sake of checking — in my head it’s tied to stopping something from going wrong, or at least making sure I didn’t leave something unsafe or unfinished.

Like with the locks, I’ll think, “If I don’t check again, maybe it isn’t really locked,” even if I already checked it. With wound supplies or a dressing area, it’s more like I’m worried I missed something and then that could lead to a problem. And at work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I keep going back because I feel like if I miss one detail, it could turn into a bigger mistake.

The frustrating part is that one check doesn’t feel like enough. I’ll do it, and I can see that it’s okay, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. That’s what’s bringing me in, really — I’m losing hours to it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, but for me it’s less like a ritual with an exact number or something, and more like I have to go through the same routine in the same kind of complete way before I can move on.

Like with my dressing supplies, I’ll check them, and then I’ll feel like maybe I didn’t really register one part of it, so I start again and go through the items in order. And with door locks, I can’t just glance at it casually — I feel like I have to check it in a certain way, like actually confirm it properly, or it doesn’t count in my head.

At work it happens with file dimensions and revision notes too. I’ll go back through them in a particular sequence, and if I get interrupted or if I don’t feel certain enough at the end, I’ll start over or repeat parts of it. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that the routine makes sense after a while. It’s more that I don’t feel allowed to stop until I’ve repeated it enough times that the anxiety drops.

That’s honestly a big part of what brought me in. I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should be straightforward, just because it doesn’t feel finished unless I do it in that specific, repeated way.

The thought just keeps coming back until I go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific physical actions in response to these repeating thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It feels like I’m pushed into doing the action, not just thinking about it. The main one is rechecking. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then check them again, and then somehow I still don’t feel finished, so I go back through the same steps. Same with the door locks — I can see that it’s locked, I can even remember locking it, but I still feel like I have to physically go back and confirm it.\n\nAt work it’s a lot of reviewing file dimensions and revision notes over and over. It’s not enough to just look once and say, “Okay, that’s done.” I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. And that can take a long time. Things that should be quick end up taking hours because I keep repeating the same checking actions until it feels… I don’t know, safe enough or complete enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating actions, like checking the locks or the wound area, aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s pretty much what it feels like. It’s not just checking for the sake of checking — in my head it’s tied to stopping something from going wrong, or at least making sure I didn’t leave something unsafe or unfinished.\n\nLike with the locks, I’ll think, “If I don’t check again, maybe it isn’t really locked,” even if I already checked it. With wound supplies or a dressing area, it’s more like I’m worried I missed something and then that could lead to a problem. And at work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I keep going back because I feel like if I miss one detail, it could turn into a bigger mistake.\n\nThe frustrating part is that one check doesn’t feel like enough. I’ll do it, and I can see that it’s okay, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. That’s what’s bringing me in, really — I’m losing hours to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done in a specific order or in a certain way to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, but for me it’s less like a ritual with an exact number or something, and more like I have to go through the same routine in the same kind of complete way before I can move on.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, I’ll check them, and then I’ll feel like maybe I didn’t really register one part of it, so I start again and go through the items in order. And with door locks, I can’t just glance at it casually — I feel like I have to check it in a certain way, like actually confirm it properly, or it doesn’t count in my head.\n\nAt work it happens with file dimensions and revision notes too. I’ll go back through them in a particular sequence, and if I get interrupted or if I don’t feel certain enough at the end, I’ll start over or repeat parts of it. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that the routine makes sense after a while. It’s more that I don’t feel allowed to stop until I’ve repeated it enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of what brought me in. I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should be straightforward, just because it doesn’t feel finished unless I do it in that specific, repeated way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yes, it’s definitely interfering now. Work is probably the clearest place I notice it. I’m an engineer, so there’s already a lot of checking built into the job, but this goes way beyond normal review. It’s frustrating because on paper it sounds like, “Just stop and go,” but in the moment it feels like I can’t move on until I’m comfortable enough, and by then the thing I wanted to do has already passed.

And I know that sounds excessive, but in the moment the anxiety is really uncomfortable, and it only drops after I’ve checked the same things repeatedly. That’s actually a big part of it. It’s not just that I decide to check something — it’s like the thought cuts in on its own and keeps coming back.\n\nLike with cleaning a wound area, even after I’ve already done it and I can look at it and know, logically, that I cleaned it, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed one tiny spot. I try to tell myself, “You already checked this, you’re being careful,” but it doesn’t really settle. It’s like reasoning with it doesn’t work the way it should. The thought just keeps coming back until I go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific physical actions in response to these repeating thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It feels like I’m pushed into doing the action, not just thinking about it. The main one is rechecking. And that can take a long time. Things that should be quick end up taking hours because I keep repeating the same checking actions until it feels… I don’t know, safe enough or complete enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating actions, like checking the locks or the wound area, aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s pretty much what it feels like. It’s not just checking for the sake of checking — in my head it’s tied to stopping something from going wrong, or at least making sure I didn’t leave something unsafe or unfinished.\n\nLike with the locks, I’ll think, “If I don’t check again, maybe it isn’t really locked,” even if I already checked it. With wound supplies or a dressing area, it’s more like I’m worried I missed something and then that could lead to a problem. And at work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I keep going back because I feel like if I miss one detail, it could turn into a bigger mistake.\n\nThe frustrating part is that one check doesn’t feel like enough. I’ll do it, and I can see that it’s okay, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. That’s what’s bringing me in, really — I’m losing hours to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done in a specific order or in a certain way to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, but for me it’s less like a ritual with an exact number or something, and more like I have to go through the same routine in the same kind of complete way before I can move on.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, I’ll check them, and then I’ll feel like maybe I didn’t really register one part of it, so I start again and go through the items in order. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really symmetrical, no — not like things have to be lined up evenly or perfectly balanced. It’s more exact in the sense that I need to feel like I checked the thing properly, in the full routine, or it doesn’t feel finished.

So with the dressing supplies, for example, it’s not that they have to look a certain way visually. It’s more like I’ll go through them, then doubt whether I really noticed each item, and then I start over. Same with door locks — I’m not making anything symmetrical, but I have to check it in the right way, like I actually confirmed it, not just glanced at it.

At work, with file dimensions or revision notes, it can feel more “exact,” I guess, because the details matter. But even then, what brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking the same things. I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops. So it’s not symmetry so much as needing the checking to feel complete enough.

It’s more exact in the sense that I need to feel like I checked the thing properly, in the full routine, or it doesn’t feel finished.\n\nSo with the dressing supplies, for example, it’s not that they have to look a certain way visually. It’s more like I’ll go through them, then doubt whether I really noticed each item, and then I start over. Same with door locks — I’m not making anything symmetrical, but I have to check it in the right way, like I actually confirmed it, not just glanced at it.\n\nAt work, with file dimensions or revision notes, it can feel more “exact,” I guess, because the details matter. But even then, what brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking the same things. I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops. It’s pretty distressing, honestly. I mean, I can tell myself logically that I’ve already checked the dressing supplies or the door lock or the file dimensions, but that doesn’t really make the feeling go away. It’s like there’s this pressure in my head that says, “You didn’t do it completely enough,” or “You might have missed something,” and then I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I go back through it again.

What brings me in is really that I’m losing hours to it. It’s not just a small annoyance anymore. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then feel unsure, then go through them again in the same order. Or I’ll check a door lock and still feel like it didn’t count unless I confirmed it the right way. At work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I can get stuck doing the same pass over and over, even when part of me knows I’m not finding anything new.

The worst part is that it only feels better after I’ve repeated the routine enough times for the anxiety to drop. And even then, it’s not like I feel good — it’s more like I finally feel allowed to stop. So yeah, I’d say it’s moderately to very distressing. It’s frustrating and exhausting, because I know these things should be straightforward, but in the moment they don’t feel straightforward at all.

I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should be straightforward, just because it doesn’t feel finished unless I do it in that specific, repeated way.\n\nDoctor: Are these repetitive checking behaviors interfering with your relationships, work, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely interfering now. Work is probably the clearest place I notice it. I’m an engineer, so there’s already a lot of checking built into the job, but this goes way beyond normal review. My project deadlines have started slipping because I’ll stay late trying to get comfortable enough to submit something. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just one more pass,” and then that turns into another pass, and another, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole evening.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s eating into the time I used to keep for myself. I used to make it to paddleboarding meetups and workouts pretty regularly, and now I’ll miss them because I’m still stuck in one of these routines or I’ve run out of time from checking things. It’s frustrating because on paper it sounds like, “Just stop and go,” but in the moment it feels like I can’t move on until I’m comfortable enough, and by then the thing I wanted to do has already passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or exact in any way when you're performing these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no — not like things have to be lined up evenly or perfectly balanced. It’s more exact in the sense that I need to feel like I checked the thing properly, in the full routine, or it doesn’t feel finished.\n\nSo with the dressing supplies, for example, it’s not that they have to look a certain way visually. It’s more like I’ll go through them, then doubt whether I really noticed each item, and then I start over. Same with door locks — I’m not making anything symmetrical, but I have to check it in the right way, like I actually confirmed it, not just glanced at it.\n\nAt work, with file dimensions or revision notes, it can feel more “exact,” I guess, because the details matter. But even then, what brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking the same things. I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops. So it’s not symmetry so much as needing the checking to feel complete enough.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these repeating thoughts and checking behaviors to be?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty distressing, honestly. I mean, I can tell myself logically that I’ve already checked the dressing supplies or the door lock or the file dimensions, but that doesn’t really make the feeling go away. It’s like there’s this pressure in my head that says, “You didn’t do it completely enough,” or “You might have missed something,” and then I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I go back through it again.\n\nWhat brings me in is really that I’m losing hours to it. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — it feels like the thought sort of cuts in, even when I’m trying to move on or focus on something totally different.

Like, if I’ve cleaned the wound area and I know I did it, I can even remember doing the steps, but then this thought comes in like, “What if you missed one tiny spot?” or “What if that one thing you didn’t notice turns into an infection?” And it doesn’t feel like a normal reminder or a useful thought. It feels more like it’s forcing its way in and demanding that I pay attention to it.

Same thing at work with CAD files. I’ll have reviewed something, and I’ll be trying to move on to the next task, and then suddenly I get this intrusive thought that maybe I missed one dimension or one small detail, and that could become a design mistake that hurts someone. Even if I try to reason with myself — like, “You already checked this,” or “There’s a process for catching errors” — it doesn’t really settle it. The thought keeps pushing back in.

So, yes, it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. It feels unwanted, and it interrupts whatever I’m trying to do until I go back and address it somehow.

So I end up spending hours on things that should take minutes, and it’s starting to get hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of it. It’s not just that I decide to check something — it’s like the thought cuts in on its own and keeps coming back.\n\nLike with cleaning a wound area, even after I’ve already done it and I can look at it and know, logically, that I cleaned it, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed one tiny spot. And then my mind jumps to, “What if that one tiny thing turns into an infection?” It feels really hard to dismiss, even if part of me knows I’m probably overestimating it.\n\nAnd it’s similar at work with CAD files. I’ll review a file or dimensions or notes, and then this thought pops back in that maybe I missed some small detail, some tiny mismatch or error, and somehow that could turn into a design mistake that affects someone or hurts someone. I try to tell myself, “You already checked this, you’re being careful,” but it doesn’t really settle. It’s like reasoning with it doesn’t work the way it should. The thought just keeps coming back until I go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific physical actions in response to these repeating thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It feels like I’m pushed into doing the action, not just thinking about it. The main one is rechecking. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then check them again, and then somehow I still don’t feel finished, so I go back through the same steps. Same with the door locks — I can see that it’s locked, I can even remember locking it, but I still feel like I have to physically go back and confirm it.\n\nAt work it’s a lot of reviewing file dimensions and revision notes over and over. It’s not enough to just look once and say, “Okay, that’s done.” I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. And that can take a long time. Things that should be quick end up taking hours because I keep repeating the same checking actions until it feels… I don’t know, safe enough or complete enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating actions, like checking the locks or the wound area, aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s pretty much what it feels like. It’s not just checking for the sake of checking — in my head it’s tied to stopping something from going wrong, or at least making sure I didn’t leave something unsafe or unfinished.\n\nLike with the locks, I’ll think, “If I don’t check again, maybe it isn’t really locked,” even if I already checked it. With wound supplies or a dressing area, it’s more like I’m worried I missed something and then that could lead to a problem. And at work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I keep going back because I feel like if I miss one detail, it could turn into a bigger mistake.\n\nThe frustrating part is that one check doesn’t feel like enough. I’ll do it, and I can see that it’s okay, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. That’s what’s bringing me in, really — I’m losing hours to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done in a specific order or in a certain way to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, but for me it’s less like a ritual with an exact number or something, and more like I have to go through the same routine in the same kind of complete way before I can move on.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, I’ll check them, and then I’ll feel like maybe I didn’t really register one part of it, so I start again and go through the items in order. And with door locks, I can’t just glance at it casually — I feel like I have to check it in a certain way, like actually confirm it properly, or it doesn’t count in my head.\n\nAt work it happens with file dimensions and revision notes too. I’ll go back through them in a particular sequence, and if I get interrupted or if I don’t feel certain enough at the end, I’ll start over or repeat parts of it. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that the routine makes sense after a while. It’s more that I don’t feel allowed to stop until I’ve repeated it enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of what brought me in. I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should be straightforward, just because it doesn’t feel finished unless I do it in that specific, repeated way.\n\nDoctor: Are these repetitive checking behaviors interfering with your relationships, work, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely interfering now. Work is probably the clearest place I notice it. I’m an engineer, so there’s already a lot of checking built into the job, but this goes way beyond normal review. My project deadlines have started slipping because I’ll stay late trying to get comfortable enough to submit something. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just one more pass,” and then that turns into another pass, and another, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole evening.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s eating into the time I used to keep for myself. I used to make it to paddleboarding meetups and workouts pretty regularly, and now I’ll miss them because I’m still stuck in one of these routines or I’ve run out of time from checking things. It’s frustrating because on paper it sounds like, “Just stop and go,” but in the moment it feels like I can’t move on until I’m comfortable enough, and by then the thing I wanted to do has already passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or exact in any way when you're performing these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no — not like things have to be lined up evenly or perfectly balanced. It’s more exact in the sense that I need to feel like I checked the thing properly, in the full routine, or it doesn’t feel finished.\n\nSo with the dressing supplies, for example, it’s not that they have to look a certain way visually. It’s more like I’ll go through them, then doubt whether I really noticed each item, and then I start over. Same with door locks — I’m not making anything symmetrical, but I have to check it in the right way, like I actually confirmed it, not just glanced at it.\n\nAt work, with file dimensions or revision notes, it can feel more “exact,” I guess, because the details matter. But even then, what brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking the same things. I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops. So it’s not symmetry so much as needing the checking to feel complete enough.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these repeating thoughts and checking behaviors to be?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty distressing, honestly. I mean, I can tell myself logically that I’ve already checked the dressing supplies or the door lock or the file dimensions, but that doesn’t really make the feeling go away. It’s like there’s this pressure in my head that says, “You didn’t do it completely enough,” or “You might have missed something,” and then I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I go back through it again.\n\nWhat brings me in is really that I’m losing hours to it. It’s not just a small annoyance anymore. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then feel unsure, then go through them again in the same order. Or I’ll check a door lock and still feel like it didn’t count unless I confirmed it the right way. At work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I can get stuck doing the same pass over and over, even when part of me knows I’m not finding anything new.\n\nThe worst part is that it only feels better after I’ve repeated the routine enough times for the anxiety to drop. And even then, it’s not like I feel good — it’s more like I finally feel allowed to stop. So yeah, I’d say it’s moderately to very distressing. It’s frustrating and exhausting, because I know these things should be straightforward, but in the moment they don’t feel straightforward at all.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — it feels like the thought sort of cuts in, even when I’m trying to move on or focus on something totally different.\n\nLike, if I’ve cleaned the wound area and I know I did it, I can even remember doing the steps, but then this thought comes in like, “What if you missed one tiny spot?” or “What if that one thing you didn’t notice turns into an infection?” And it doesn’t feel like a normal reminder or a useful thought. It feels more like it’s forcing its way in and demanding that I pay attention to it.\n\nSame thing at work with CAD files. I’ll have reviewed something, and I’ll be trying to move on to the next task, and then suddenly I get this intrusive thought that maybe I missed one dimension or one small detail, and that could become a design mistake that hurts someone. Even if I try to reason with myself — like, “You already checked this,” or “There’s a process for catching errors” — it doesn’t really settle it. The thought keeps pushing back in.\n\nSo, yes, it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. It feels unwanted, and it interrupts whatever I’m trying to do until I go back and address it somehow.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Or I’ll check a door lock and still feel like it didn’t count unless I confirmed it the right way. At work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I can get stuck doing the same pass over and over, even when part of me knows I’m not finding anything new.\n\nThe worst part is that it only feels better after I’ve repeated the routine enough times for the anxiety to drop. And even then, it’s not like I feel good — it’s more like I finally feel allowed to stop. So yeah, I’d say it’s moderately to very distressing. It’s frustrating and exhausting, because I know these things should be straightforward, but in the moment they don’t feel straightforward at all.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — it feels like the thought sort of cuts in, even when I’m trying to move on or focus on something totally different.\n\nLike, if I’ve cleaned the wound area and I know I did it, I can even remember doing the steps, but then this thought comes in like, “What if you missed one tiny spot?” or “What if that one thing you didn’t notice turns into an infection?” And it doesn’t feel like a normal reminder or a useful thought. It feels more like it’s forcing its way in and demanding that I pay attention to it.\n\nSame thing at work with CAD files. I’ll have reviewed something, and I’ll be trying to move on to the next task, and then suddenly I get this intrusive thought that maybe I missed one dimension or one small detail, and that could become a design mistake that hurts someone. Even if I try to reason with myself — like, “You already checked this,” or “There’s a process for catching errors” — it doesn’t really settle it. The thought keeps pushing back in.\n\nSo, yes, it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. It feels unwanted, and it interrupts whatever I’m trying to do until I go back and address it somehow.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like these repeating thoughts are a part of yourself or do they feel alien, like they're coming from outside of you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 25 / engineer / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Obsessions\nmanifestation: \"Even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area or reviewed a CAD file, a thought keeps cutting in that I missed one tiny thing and it’s going to turn into an infection or a design mistake that hurts someone, and I can’t just reason it away.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They don’t feel like they’re coming from outside of me, no. It’s not like I hear them as a voice or feel like someone else is putting them there. They feel like my own thoughts, which is part of what makes it frustrating, I guess.

But they also don’t feel like thoughts I want to be having. It’s like they come from inside my own mind, but they’re intrusive — like they barge in and take over. I can know, for example, that I cleaned the wound area. I can picture myself doing it. Or I can know I reviewed the CAD file and went through the dimensions already. But then this thought cuts in anyway: “What if you missed one tiny thing?” And then it jumps straight to, “What if that turns into an infection?” or “What if that becomes a design mistake that hurts someone?”

So I’d say they feel like mine, but not really like me, if that makes sense. They’re not alien in the sense of coming from outside, but they feel unwanted and hard to shut off. I try to reason with them — like, “No, I already checked,” or “That’s not likely” — but it doesn’t really make them go away. They keep pressing until I respond to them somehow.

And I know that sounds excessive, but in the moment the anxiety is really uncomfortable, and it only drops after I’ve checked the same things repeatedly. So I end up spending hours on things that should take minutes, and it’s starting to get hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s not just that I decide to check something — it’s like the thought cuts in on its own and keeps coming back.\n\nLike with cleaning a wound area, even after I’ve already done it and I can look at it and know, logically, that I cleaned it, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed one tiny spot. And then my mind jumps to, “What if that one tiny thing turns into an infection?” It feels really hard to dismiss, even if part of me knows I’m probably overestimating it.\n\nAnd it’s similar at work with CAD files. And that can take a long time. Things that should be quick end up taking hours because I keep repeating the same checking actions until it feels… I don’t know, safe enough or complete enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating actions, like checking the locks or the wound area, aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s pretty much what it feels like. It’s not just checking for the sake of checking — in my head it’s tied to stopping something from going wrong, or at least making sure I didn’t leave something unsafe or unfinished.\n\nLike with the locks, I’ll think, “If I don’t check again, maybe it isn’t really locked,” even if I already checked it. With wound supplies or a dressing area, it’s more like I’m worried I missed something and then that could lead to a problem. And at work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I keep going back because I feel like if I miss one detail, it could turn into a bigger mistake.\n\nThe frustrating part is that one check doesn’t feel like enough. I’ll do it, and I can see that it’s okay, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. That’s what’s bringing me in, really — I’m losing hours to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done in a specific order or in a certain way to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, but for me it’s less like a ritual with an exact number or something, and more like I have to go through the same routine in the same kind of complete way before I can move on.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, I’ll check them, and then I’ll feel like maybe I didn’t really register one part of it, so I start again and go through the items in order. And with door locks, I can’t just glance at it casually — I feel like I have to check it in a certain way, like actually confirm it properly, or it doesn’t count in my head.\n\nAt work it happens with file dimensions and revision notes too. I’ll go back through them in a particular sequence, and if I get interrupted or if I don’t feel certain enough at the end, I’ll start over or repeat parts of it. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that the routine makes sense after a while. It’s more that I don’t feel allowed to stop until I’ve repeated it enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of what brought me in. I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should be straightforward, just because it doesn’t feel finished unless I do it in that specific, repeated way.\n\nDoctor: Are these repetitive checking behaviors interfering with your relationships, work, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely interfering now. Work is probably the clearest place I notice it. I’m an engineer, so there’s already a lot of checking built into the job, but this goes way beyond normal review. My project deadlines have started slipping because I’ll stay late trying to get comfortable enough to submit something. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just one more pass,” and then that turns into another pass, and another, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole evening.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s eating into the time I used to keep for myself. I used to make it to paddleboarding meetups and workouts pretty regularly, and now I’ll miss them because I’m still stuck in one of these routines or I’ve run out of time from checking things. It’s frustrating because on paper it sounds like, “Just stop and go,” but in the moment it feels like I can’t move on until I’m comfortable enough, and by then the thing I wanted to do has already passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or exact in any way when you're performing these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no — not like things have to be lined up evenly or perfectly balanced. It’s more exact in the sense that I need to feel like I checked the thing properly, in the full routine, or it doesn’t feel finished.\n\nSo with the dressing supplies, for example, it’s not that they have to look a certain way visually. It’s more like I’ll go through them, then doubt whether I really noticed each item, and then I start over. Same with door locks — I’m not making anything symmetrical, but I have to check it in the right way, like I actually confirmed it, not just glanced at it.\n\nAt work, with file dimensions or revision notes, it can feel more “exact,” I guess, because the details matter. But even then, what brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking the same things. I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops. So it’s not symmetry so much as needing the checking to feel complete enough.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these repeating thoughts and checking behaviors to be?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty distressing, honestly. It’s like there’s this pressure in my head that says, “You didn’t do it completely enough,” or “You might have missed something,” and then I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I go back through it again.\n\nWhat brings me in is really that I’m losing hours to it. It’s not just a small annoyance anymore. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then feel unsure, then go through them again in the same order. Or I’ll check a door lock and still feel like it didn’t count unless I confirmed it the right way. At work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I can get stuck doing the same pass over and over, even when part of me knows I’m not finding anything new.\n\nThe worst part is that it only feels better after I’ve repeated the routine enough times for the anxiety to drop. I mean, I can tell myself logically that I’ve already checked the dressing supplies or the door lock or the file dimensions, but that doesn’t really make the feeling go away. It’s like there’s this pressure in my head that says, “You didn’t do it completely enough,” or “You might have missed something,” and then I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I go back through it again.\n\nWhat brings me in is really that I’m losing hours to it. It’s not just a small annoyance anymore. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then feel unsure, then go through them again in the same order. Or I’ll check a door lock and still feel like it didn’t count unless I confirmed it the right way. At work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I can get stuck doing the same pass over and over, even when part of me knows I’m not finding anything new.\n\nThe worst part is that it only feels better after I’ve repeated the routine enough times for the anxiety to drop. And even then, it’s not like I feel good — it’s more like I finally feel allowed to stop. So yeah, I’d say it’s moderately to very distressing. It’s frustrating and exhausting, because I know these things should be straightforward, but in the moment they don’t feel straightforward at all.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — it feels like the thought sort of cuts in, even when I’m trying to move on or focus on something totally different.\n\nLike, if I’ve cleaned the wound area and I know I did it, I can even remember doing the steps, but then this thought comes in like, “What if you missed one tiny spot?” or “What if that one thing you didn’t notice turns into an infection?” And it doesn’t feel like a normal reminder or a useful thought. It feels more like it’s forcing its way in and demanding that I pay attention to it.\n\nSame thing at work with CAD files. Even if I try to reason with myself — like, “You already checked this,” or “There’s a process for catching errors” — it doesn’t really settle it. The thought keeps pushing back in.\n\nSo, yes, it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. It feels unwanted, and it interrupts whatever I’m trying to do until I go back and address it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repeating thoughts are a part of yourself or do they feel alien, like they're coming from outside of you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel like they’re coming from outside of me, no. It’s not like I hear them as a voice or feel like someone else is putting them there. They feel like my own thoughts, which is part of what makes it frustrating, I guess.\n\nBut they also don’t feel like thoughts I want to be having. It’s like they come from inside my own mind, but they’re intrusive — like they barge in and take over. I can know, for example, that I cleaned the wound area. I can picture myself doing it. Or I can know I reviewed the CAD file and went through the dimensions already. But then this thought cuts in anyway: “What if you missed one tiny thing?” And then it jumps straight to, “What if that turns into an infection?” or “What if that becomes a design mistake that hurts someone?”\n\nSo I’d say they feel like mine, but not really like me, if that makes sense. They’re not alien in the sense of coming from outside, but they feel unwanted and hard to shut off. I try to reason with them — like, “No, I already checked,” or “That’s not likely” — but it doesn’t really make them go away. They keep pressing until I respond to them somehow.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like your checking behaviors are driven by a need to make things symmetrical or perfect in some way?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really symmetrical, no. I mean, it can look like I’m trying to do things “perfectly” from the outside, because I’ll go through the same checks over and over, but it doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because something has to be evenly lined up or arranged just right.

It’s more that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety drops. Like with my dressing supplies, I’ll look through them, and then there’s this doubt that maybe I didn’t really confirm everything, so I go back through the same routine again. Same with door locks, or file dimensions, or revision notes at work. It’s not that they need to feel symmetrical — it’s that I don’t trust that I’m done.

And I know that sounds kind of circular, because the checking is supposed to reassure me, but it only reassures me for a little while, or only after I’ve repeated it enough times. That’s what’s been getting bad enough that I came in — I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should take a few minutes, because until the anxiety comes down, it feels like I can’t just leave it alone.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing is I’m losing a lot of time to checking things over and over. It’s not just one thing, either. Like, I’ll check my dressing supplies, or the door locks, or at work I’ll keep going back over file dimensions and revision notes, and even when I know I’ve already looked, I don’t feel like I can just move on.\n\nIt’s like there’s this feeling that if I don’t go through the whole routine properly, or enough times, something is still unfinished or unsafe or wrong. And I know that sounds excessive, but in the moment the anxiety is really uncomfortable, and it only drops after I’ve checked the same things repeatedly. So I end up spending hours on things that should take minutes, and it’s starting to get hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of it. It’s not just that I decide to check something — it’s like the thought cuts in on its own and keeps coming back.\n\nLike with cleaning a wound area, even after I’ve already done it and I can look at it and know, logically, that I cleaned it, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed one tiny spot. And then my mind jumps to, “What if that one tiny thing turns into an infection?” It feels really hard to dismiss, even if part of me knows I’m probably overestimating it.\n\nAnd it’s similar at work with CAD files. I’ll review a file or dimensions or notes, and then this thought pops back in that maybe I missed some small detail, some tiny mismatch or error, and somehow that could turn into a design mistake that affects someone or hurts someone. I try to tell myself, “You already checked this, you’re being careful,” but it doesn’t really settle. It’s like reasoning with it doesn’t work the way it should. The thought just keeps coming back until I go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific physical actions in response to these repeating thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It feels like I’m pushed into doing the action, not just thinking about it. The main one is rechecking. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then check them again, and then somehow I still don’t feel finished, so I go back through the same steps. Same with the door locks — I can see that it’s locked, I can even remember locking it, but I still feel like I have to physically go back and confirm it.\n\nAt work it’s a lot of reviewing file dimensions and revision notes over and over. It’s not enough to just look once and say, “Okay, that’s done.” I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. And that can take a long time. Things that should be quick end up taking hours because I keep repeating the same checking actions until it feels… I don’t know, safe enough or complete enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating actions, like checking the locks or the wound area, aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s pretty much what it feels like. It’s not just checking for the sake of checking — in my head it’s tied to stopping something from going wrong, or at least making sure I didn’t leave something unsafe or unfinished.\n\nLike with the locks, I’ll think, “If I don’t check again, maybe it isn’t really locked,” even if I already checked it. With wound supplies or a dressing area, it’s more like I’m worried I missed something and then that could lead to a problem. And at work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I keep going back because I feel like if I miss one detail, it could turn into a bigger mistake.\n\nThe frustrating part is that one check doesn’t feel like enough. I’ll do it, and I can see that it’s okay, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. That’s what’s bringing me in, really — I’m losing hours to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done in a specific order or in a certain way to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, but for me it’s less like a ritual with an exact number or something, and more like I have to go through the same routine in the same kind of complete way before I can move on.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, I’ll check them, and then I’ll feel like maybe I didn’t really register one part of it, so I start again and go through the items in order. And with door locks, I can’t just glance at it casually — I feel like I have to check it in a certain way, like actually confirm it properly, or it doesn’t count in my head.\n\nAt work it happens with file dimensions and revision notes too. I’ll go back through them in a particular sequence, and if I get interrupted or if I don’t feel certain enough at the end, I’ll start over or repeat parts of it. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that the routine makes sense after a while. It’s more that I don’t feel allowed to stop until I’ve repeated it enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of what brought me in. I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should be straightforward, just because it doesn’t feel finished unless I do it in that specific, repeated way.\n\nDoctor: Are these repetitive checking behaviors interfering with your relationships, work, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely interfering now. Work is probably the clearest place I notice it. I’m an engineer, so there’s already a lot of checking built into the job, but this goes way beyond normal review. My project deadlines have started slipping because I’ll stay late trying to get comfortable enough to submit something. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just one more pass,” and then that turns into another pass, and another, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole evening.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s eating into the time I used to keep for myself. I used to make it to paddleboarding meetups and workouts pretty regularly, and now I’ll miss them because I’m still stuck in one of these routines or I’ve run out of time from checking things. It’s frustrating because on paper it sounds like, “Just stop and go,” but in the moment it feels like I can’t move on until I’m comfortable enough, and by then the thing I wanted to do has already passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or exact in any way when you're performing these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no — not like things have to be lined up evenly or perfectly balanced. It’s more exact in the sense that I need to feel like I checked the thing properly, in the full routine, or it doesn’t feel finished.\n\nSo with the dressing supplies, for example, it’s not that they have to look a certain way visually. It’s more like I’ll go through them, then doubt whether I really noticed each item, and then I start over. Same with door locks — I’m not making anything symmetrical, but I have to check it in the right way, like I actually confirmed it, not just glanced at it.\n\nAt work, with file dimensions or revision notes, it can feel more “exact,” I guess, because the details matter. But even then, what brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking the same things. I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops. So it’s not symmetry so much as needing the checking to feel complete enough.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these repeating thoughts and checking behaviors to be?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty distressing, honestly. I mean, I can tell myself logically that I’ve already checked the dressing supplies or the door lock or the file dimensions, but that doesn’t really make the feeling go away. It’s like there’s this pressure in my head that says, “You didn’t do it completely enough,” or “You might have missed something,” and then I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I go back through it again.\n\nWhat brings me in is really that I’m losing hours to it. It’s not just a small annoyance anymore. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then feel unsure, then go through them again in the same order. Or I’ll check a door lock and still feel like it didn’t count unless I confirmed it the right way. At work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I can get stuck doing the same pass over and over, even when part of me knows I’m not finding anything new.\n\nThe worst part is that it only feels better after I’ve repeated the routine enough times for the anxiety to drop. And even then, it’s not like I feel good — it’s more like I finally feel allowed to stop. So yeah, I’d say it’s moderately to very distressing. It’s frustrating and exhausting, because I know these things should be straightforward, but in the moment they don’t feel straightforward at all.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — it feels like the thought sort of cuts in, even when I’m trying to move on or focus on something totally different.\n\nLike, if I’ve cleaned the wound area and I know I did it, I can even remember doing the steps, but then this thought comes in like, “What if you missed one tiny spot?” or “What if that one thing you didn’t notice turns into an infection?” And it doesn’t feel like a normal reminder or a useful thought. It feels more like it’s forcing its way in and demanding that I pay attention to it.\n\nSame thing at work with CAD files. I’ll have reviewed something, and I’ll be trying to move on to the next task, and then suddenly I get this intrusive thought that maybe I missed one dimension or one small detail, and that could become a design mistake that hurts someone. Even if I try to reason with myself — like, “You already checked this,” or “There’s a process for catching errors” — it doesn’t really settle it. The thought keeps pushing back in.\n\nSo, yes, it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. It feels unwanted, and it interrupts whatever I’m trying to do until I go back and address it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repeating thoughts are a part of yourself or do they feel alien, like they're coming from outside of you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel like they’re coming from outside of me, no. Or I can know I reviewed the CAD file and went through the dimensions already. So I end up spending hours on things that should take minutes, and it’s starting to get hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of it. It’s not just that I decide to check something — it’s like the thought cuts in on its own and keeps coming back.\n\nLike with cleaning a wound area, even after I’ve already done it and I can look at it and know, logically, that I cleaned it, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed one tiny spot. And then my mind jumps to, “What if that one tiny thing turns into an infection?” It feels really hard to dismiss, even if part of me knows I’m probably overestimating it.\n\nAnd it’s similar at work with CAD files. I’ll review a file or dimensions or notes, and then this thought pops back in that maybe I missed some small detail, some tiny mismatch or error, and somehow that could turn into a design mistake that affects someone or hurts someone. I try to tell myself, “You already checked this, you’re being careful,” but it doesn’t really settle. It’s like reasoning with it doesn’t work the way it should. The thought just keeps coming back until I go over it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific physical actions in response to these repeating thoughts?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. It feels like I’m pushed into doing the action, not just thinking about it. The main one is rechecking. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then check them again, and then somehow I still don’t feel finished, so I go back through the same steps. Same with the door locks — I can see that it’s locked, I can even remember locking it, but I still feel like I have to physically go back and confirm it.\n\nAt work it’s a lot of reviewing file dimensions and revision notes over and over. It’s not enough to just look once and say, “Okay, that’s done.” I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. And that can take a long time. Things that should be quick end up taking hours because I keep repeating the same checking actions until it feels… I don’t know, safe enough or complete enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating actions, like checking the locks or the wound area, aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s pretty much what it feels like. It’s not just checking for the sake of checking — in my head it’s tied to stopping something from going wrong, or at least making sure I didn’t leave something unsafe or unfinished.\n\nLike with the locks, I’ll think, “If I don’t check again, maybe it isn’t really locked,” even if I already checked it. With wound supplies or a dressing area, it’s more like I’m worried I missed something and then that could lead to a problem. And at work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I keep going back because I feel like if I miss one detail, it could turn into a bigger mistake.\n\nThe frustrating part is that one check doesn’t feel like enough. I’ll do it, and I can see that it’s okay, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. That’s what’s bringing me in, really — I’m losing hours to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done in a specific order or in a certain way to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, but for me it’s less like a ritual with an exact number or something, and more like I have to go through the same routine in the same kind of complete way before I can move on.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, I’ll check them, and then I’ll feel like maybe I didn’t really register one part of it, so I start again and go through the items in order. And with door locks, I can’t just glance at it casually — I feel like I have to check it in a certain way, like actually confirm it properly, or it doesn’t count in my head.\n\nAt work it happens with file dimensions and revision notes too. I’ll go back through them in a particular sequence, and if I get interrupted or if I don’t feel certain enough at the end, I’ll start over or repeat parts of it. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that the routine makes sense after a while. It’s more that I don’t feel allowed to stop until I’ve repeated it enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of what brought me in. I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should be straightforward, just because it doesn’t feel finished unless I do it in that specific, repeated way.\n\nDoctor: Are these repetitive checking behaviors interfering with your relationships, work, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely interfering now. Work is probably the clearest place I notice it. I’m an engineer, so there’s already a lot of checking built into the job, but this goes way beyond normal review. My project deadlines have started slipping because I’ll stay late trying to get comfortable enough to submit something. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just one more pass,” and then that turns into another pass, and another, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole evening.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s eating into the time I used to keep for myself. I used to make it to paddleboarding meetups and workouts pretty regularly, and now I’ll miss them because I’m still stuck in one of these routines or I’ve run out of time from checking things. It’s frustrating because on paper it sounds like, “Just stop and go,” but in the moment it feels like I can’t move on until I’m comfortable enough, and by then the thing I wanted to do has already passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or exact in any way when you're performing these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no — not like things have to be lined up evenly or perfectly balanced. It’s more exact in the sense that I need to feel like I checked the thing properly, in the full routine, or it doesn’t feel finished.\n\nSo with the dressing supplies, for example, it’s not that they have to look a certain way visually. It’s more like I’ll go through them, then doubt whether I really noticed each item, and then I start over. Same with door locks — I’m not making anything symmetrical, but I have to check it in the right way, like I actually confirmed it, not just glanced at it.\n\nAt work, with file dimensions or revision notes, it can feel more “exact,” I guess, because the details matter. But even then, what brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking the same things. I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops. So it’s not symmetry so much as needing the checking to feel complete enough.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these repeating thoughts and checking behaviors to be?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty distressing, honestly. I mean, I can tell myself logically that I’ve already checked the dressing supplies or the door lock or the file dimensions, but that doesn’t really make the feeling go away. It’s like there’s this pressure in my head that says, “You didn’t do it completely enough,” or “You might have missed something,” and then I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I go back through it again.\n\nWhat brings me in is really that I’m losing hours to it. It’s not just a small annoyance anymore. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then feel unsure, then go through them again in the same order. Or I’ll check a door lock and still feel like it didn’t count unless I confirmed it the right way. At work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I can get stuck doing the same pass over and over, even when part of me knows I’m not finding anything new.\n\nThe worst part is that it only feels better after I’ve repeated the routine enough times for the anxiety to drop. And even then, it’s not like I feel good — it’s more like I finally feel allowed to stop. So yeah, I’d say it’s moderately to very distressing. It’s frustrating and exhausting, because I know these things should be straightforward, but in the moment they don’t feel straightforward at all.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — it feels like the thought sort of cuts in, even when I’m trying to move on or focus on something totally different.\n\nLike, if I’ve cleaned the wound area and I know I did it, I can even remember doing the steps, but then this thought comes in like, “What if you missed one tiny spot?” or “What if that one thing you didn’t notice turns into an infection?” And it doesn’t feel like a normal reminder or a useful thought. It feels more like it’s forcing its way in and demanding that I pay attention to it.\n\nSame thing at work with CAD files. I’ll have reviewed something, and I’ll be trying to move on to the next task, and then suddenly I get this intrusive thought that maybe I missed one dimension or one small detail, and that could become a design mistake that hurts someone. Even if I try to reason with myself — like, “You already checked this,” or “There’s a process for catching errors” — it doesn’t really settle it. The thought keeps pushing back in.\n\nSo, yes, it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. It feels unwanted, and it interrupts whatever I’m trying to do until I go back and address it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repeating thoughts are a part of yourself or do they feel alien, like they're coming from outside of you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel like they’re coming from outside of me, no. It’s not like I hear them as a voice or feel like someone else is putting them there. They feel like my own thoughts, which is part of what makes it frustrating, I guess.\n\nBut they also don’t feel like thoughts I want to be having. It’s like they come from inside my own mind, but they’re intrusive — like they barge in and take over. I can know, for example, that I cleaned the wound area. I can picture myself doing it. Or I can know I reviewed the CAD file and went through the dimensions already. But then this thought cuts in anyway: “What if you missed one tiny thing?” And then it jumps straight to, “What if that turns into an infection?” or “What if that becomes a design mistake that hurts someone?”\n\nSo I’d say they feel like mine, but not really like me, if that makes sense. They’re not alien in the sense of coming from outside, but they feel unwanted and hard to shut off. I try to reason with them — like, “No, I already checked,” or “That’s not likely” — but it doesn’t really make them go away. They keep pressing until I respond to them somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are driven by a need to make things symmetrical or perfect in some way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. I mean, it can look like I’m trying to do things “perfectly” from the outside, because I’ll go through the same checks over and over, but it doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because something has to be evenly lined up or arranged just right.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety drops. Like with my dressing supplies, I’ll look through them, and then there’s this doubt that maybe I didn’t really confirm everything, so I go back through the same routine again. Same with door locks, or file dimensions, or revision notes at work. It’s not that they need to feel symmetrical — it’s that I don’t trust that I’m done.\n\nAnd I know that sounds kind of circular, because the checking is supposed to reassure me, but it only reassures me for a little while, or only after I’ve repeated it enough times. That’s what’s been getting bad enough that I came in — I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should take a few minutes, because until the anxiety comes down, it feels like I can’t just leave it alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sometimes, yes, but it’s not always like a clear picture, if that makes sense. It’s more like the thought comes with a quick mental “scene” or flash of what could happen.

With the wound stuff, even after I know I cleaned the area, I’ll suddenly get this idea that I missed one tiny spot, and then my mind sort of jumps to an image of it getting infected. Not like a hallucination or anything — I know it’s in my head — but it’s vivid enough that it makes the worry feel more real. I’ll picture that I overlooked something small, and then it turns into something worse because I didn’t catch it.

And at work, with CAD files, it’s similar. I might have reviewed the file already, but then the thought cuts in that maybe one tiny dimension or detail is wrong. And then I sort of picture the design mistake carrying forward and someone getting hurt because I didn’t catch it. It’s not always a full detailed image, sometimes it’s more like a mental impression or scenario, but it comes in very fast and it’s hard to just dismiss.

The frustrating part is that I can tell myself, “You already checked this,” but the image or scenario still kind of hangs there, like evidence against me, even though I know logically it’s not really evidence.
