It’s not enough to just look once and say, “Okay, that’s done.” I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. And that can take a long time. Things that should be quick end up taking hours because I keep repeating the same checking actions until it feels… I don’t know, safe enough or complete enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating actions, like checking the locks or the wound area, aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s pretty much what it feels like. It’s not just checking for the sake of checking — in my head it’s tied to stopping something from going wrong, or at least making sure I didn’t leave something unsafe or unfinished.\n\nLike with the locks, I’ll think, “If I don’t check again, maybe it isn’t really locked,” even if I already checked it. With wound supplies or a dressing area, it’s more like I’m worried I missed something and then that could lead to a problem. And at work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I keep going back because I feel like if I miss one detail, it could turn into a bigger mistake.\n\nThe frustrating part is that one check doesn’t feel like enough. I’ll do it, and I can see that it’s okay, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. That’s what’s bringing me in, really — I’m losing hours to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done in a specific order or in a certain way to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, but for me it’s less like a ritual with an exact number or something, and more like I have to go through the same routine in the same kind of complete way before I can move on.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, I’ll check them, and then I’ll feel like maybe I didn’t really register one part of it, so I start again and go through the items in order. And with door locks, I can’t just glance at it casually — I feel like I have to check it in a certain way, like actually confirm it properly, or it doesn’t count in my head.\n\nAt work it happens with file dimensions and revision notes too. I’ll go back through them in a particular sequence, and if I get interrupted or if I don’t feel certain enough at the end, I’ll start over or repeat parts of it. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that the routine makes sense after a while. It’s more that I don’t feel allowed to stop until I’ve repeated it enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of what brought me in. I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should be straightforward, just because it doesn’t feel finished unless I do it in that specific, repeated way.\n\nDoctor: Are these repetitive checking behaviors interfering with your relationships, work, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely interfering now. Work is probably the clearest place I notice it. I’m an engineer, so there’s already a lot of checking built into the job, but this goes way beyond normal review. My project deadlines have started slipping because I’ll stay late trying to get comfortable enough to submit something. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just one more pass,” and then that turns into another pass, and another, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole evening.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s eating into the time I used to keep for myself. I used to make it to paddleboarding meetups and workouts pretty regularly, and now I’ll miss them because I’m still stuck in one of these routines or I’ve run out of time from checking things. It’s frustrating because on paper it sounds like, “Just stop and go,” but in the moment it feels like I can’t move on until I’m comfortable enough, and by then the thing I wanted to do has already passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or exact in any way when you're performing these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no — not like things have to be lined up evenly or perfectly balanced. It’s more exact in the sense that I need to feel like I checked the thing properly, in the full routine, or it doesn’t feel finished.\n\nSo with the dressing supplies, for example, it’s not that they have to look a certain way visually. It’s more like I’ll go through them, then doubt whether I really noticed each item, and then I start over. Same with door locks — I’m not making anything symmetrical, but I have to check it in the right way, like I actually confirmed it, not just glanced at it.\n\nAt work, with file dimensions or revision notes, it can feel more “exact,” I guess, because the details matter. But even then, what brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking the same things. I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops. So it’s not symmetry so much as needing the checking to feel complete enough.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these repeating thoughts and checking behaviors to be?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty distressing, honestly. I mean, I can tell myself logically that I’ve already checked the dressing supplies or the door lock or the file dimensions, but that doesn’t really make the feeling go away. It’s like there’s this pressure in my head that says, “You didn’t do it completely enough,” or “You might have missed something,” and then I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I go back through it again.\n\nWhat brings me in is really that I’m losing hours to it. It’s not just a small annoyance anymore. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then feel unsure, then go through them again in the same order. Or I’ll check a door lock and still feel like it didn’t count unless I confirmed it the right way. At work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I can get stuck doing the same pass over and over, even when part of me knows I’m not finding anything new.\n\nThe worst part is that it only feels better after I’ve repeated the routine enough times for the anxiety to drop. And even then, it’s not like I feel good — it’s more like I finally feel allowed to stop. So yeah, I’d say it’s moderately to very distressing. It’s frustrating and exhausting, because I know these things should be straightforward, but in the moment they don’t feel straightforward at all.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — it feels like the thought sort of cuts in, even when I’m trying to move on or focus on something totally different.\n\nLike, if I’ve cleaned the wound area and I know I did it, I can even remember doing the steps, but then this thought comes in like, “What if you missed one tiny spot?” or “What if that one thing you didn’t notice turns into an infection?” And it doesn’t feel like a normal reminder or a useful thought. It feels more like it’s forcing its way in and demanding that I pay attention to it.\n\nSame thing at work with CAD files. I’ll have reviewed something, and I’ll be trying to move on to the next task, and then suddenly I get this intrusive thought that maybe I missed one dimension or one small detail, and that could become a design mistake that hurts someone. Even if I try to reason with myself — like, “You already checked this,” or “There’s a process for catching errors” — it doesn’t really settle it. The thought keeps pushing back in.\n\nSo, yes, it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. It feels unwanted, and it interrupts whatever I’m trying to do until I go back and address it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repeating thoughts are a part of yourself or do they feel alien, like they're coming from outside of you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel like they’re coming from outside of me, no. It’s not like I hear them as a voice or feel like someone else is putting them there. They feel like my own thoughts, which is part of what makes it frustrating, I guess.\n\nBut they also don’t feel like thoughts I want to be having. It’s like they come from inside my own mind, but they’re intrusive — like they barge in and take over. I can know, for example, that I cleaned the wound area. I can picture myself doing it. Or I can know I reviewed the CAD file and went through the dimensions already. But then this thought cuts in anyway: “What if you missed one tiny thing?” And then it jumps straight to, “What if that turns into an infection?” or “What if that becomes a design mistake that hurts someone?”\n\nSo I’d say they feel like mine, but not really like me, if that makes sense. They’re not alien in the sense of coming from outside, but they feel unwanted and hard to shut off. I try to reason with them — like, “No, I already checked,” or “That’s not likely” — but it doesn’t really make them go away. They keep pressing until I respond to them somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are driven by a need to make things symmetrical or perfect in some way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. I mean, it can look like I’m trying to do things “perfectly” from the outside, because I’ll go through the same checks over and over, but it doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because something has to be evenly lined up or arranged just right.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety drops. Like with my dressing supplies, I’ll look through them, and then there’s this doubt that maybe I didn’t really confirm everything, so I go back through the same routine again. Same with door locks, or file dimensions, or revision notes at work. It’s not that they need to feel symmetrical — it’s that I don’t trust that I’m done.\n\nAnd I know that sounds kind of circular, because the checking is supposed to reassure me, but it only reassures me for a little while, or only after I’ve repeated it enough times. That’s what’s been getting bad enough that I came in — I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should take a few minutes, because until the anxiety comes down, it feels like I can’t just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: When these repeating thoughts come into your mind, are they accompanied by any specific mental images or pictures?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes, but it’s not always like a clear picture, if that makes sense. It’s more like the thought comes with a quick mental “scene” or flash of what could happen.\n\nWith the wound stuff, even after I know I cleaned the area, I’ll suddenly get this idea that I missed one tiny spot, and then my mind sort of jumps to an image of it getting infected. Not like a hallucination or anything — I know it’s in my head — but it’s vivid enough that it makes the worry feel more real. I’ll picture that I overlooked something small, and then it turns into something worse because I didn’t catch it.\n\nAnd at work, with CAD files, it’s similar. I might have reviewed the file already, but then the thought cuts in that maybe one tiny dimension or detail is wrong. And then I sort of picture the design mistake carrying forward and someone getting hurt because I didn’t catch it. It’s not always a full detailed image, sometimes it’s more like a mental impression or scenario, but it comes in very fast and it’s hard to just dismiss.\n\nThe frustrating part is that I can tell myself, “You already checked this,” but the image or scenario still kind of hangs there, like evidence against me, even though I know logically it’s not really evidence.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s not that they need to feel symmetrical — it’s that I don’t trust that I’m done.\n\nAnd I know that sounds kind of circular, because the checking is supposed to reassure me, but it only reassures me for a little while, or only after I’ve repeated it enough times. That’s what’s been getting bad enough that I came in — I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should take a few minutes, because until the anxiety comes down, it feels like I can’t just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: When these repeating thoughts come into your mind, are they accompanied by any specific mental images or pictures?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes, but it’s not always like a clear picture, if that makes sense. It’s more like the thought comes with a quick mental “scene” or flash of what could happen.\n\nWith the wound stuff, even after I know I cleaned the area, I’ll suddenly get this idea that I missed one tiny spot, and then my mind sort of jumps to an image of it getting infected. Not like a hallucination or anything — I know it’s in my head — but it’s vivid enough that it makes the worry feel more real. I’ll picture that I overlooked something small, and then it turns into something worse because I didn’t catch it.\n\nAnd at work, with CAD files, it’s similar. I might have reviewed the file already, but then the thought cuts in that maybe one tiny dimension or detail is wrong. And then I sort of picture the design mistake carrying forward and someone getting hurt because I didn’t catch it. It’s not always a full detailed image, sometimes it’s more like a mental impression or scenario, but it comes in very fast and it’s hard to just dismiss.\n\nThe frustrating part is that I can tell myself, “You already checked this,” but the image or scenario still kind of hangs there, like evidence against me, even though I know logically it’s not really evidence.\n\n[Last Question]\nAre your repeating thoughts mostly about the fear of making a mistake or causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that’s probably the main theme of them. It’s not just a vague worry like, “Oh, I hope I did that right.” It’s more like, even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area, or I know I’ve already reviewed a CAD file, this thought cuts in really sharply that I missed one tiny thing.

And then it doesn’t stay small. It sort of escalates immediately in my head. Like with the wound, it becomes, “What if that one thing turns into an infection?” And with work, it’s, “What if that tiny missed detail becomes a design mistake and someone gets hurt because of it?” So, yes — a lot of it is about the fear that I’ll make some mistake, even a small one, and it’ll lead to harm.

The part that bothers me is that I can’t just reason it away. I can tell myself, “You already checked,” or “You cleaned it,” or “You reviewed that file carefully,” but the thought doesn’t really respond to logic. It keeps pushing like, “But what if you didn’t?” And then I feel stuck on that possibility.

Same with the door locks — I can see that it’s locked, I can even remember locking it, but I still feel like I have to physically go back and confirm it.\n\nAt work it’s a lot of reviewing file dimensions and revision notes over and over. And that can take a long time. Things that should be quick end up taking hours because I keep repeating the same checking actions until it feels… I don’t know, safe enough or complete enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating actions, like checking the locks or the wound area, aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s pretty much what it feels like. It’s not just checking for the sake of checking — in my head it’s tied to stopping something from going wrong, or at least making sure I didn’t leave something unsafe or unfinished.\n\nLike with the locks, I’ll think, “If I don’t check again, maybe it isn’t really locked,” even if I already checked it. With wound supplies or a dressing area, it’s more like I’m worried I missed something and then that could lead to a problem. I’ll do it, and I can see that it’s okay, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. That’s what’s bringing me in, really — I’m losing hours to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done in a specific order or in a certain way to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, but for me it’s less like a ritual with an exact number or something, and more like I have to go through the same routine in the same kind of complete way before I can move on.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, I’ll check them, and then I’ll feel like maybe I didn’t really register one part of it, so I start again and go through the items in order. And with door locks, I can’t just glance at it casually — I feel like I have to check it in a certain way, like actually confirm it properly, or it doesn’t count in my head.\n\nAt work it happens with file dimensions and revision notes too. I’ll go back through them in a particular sequence, and if I get interrupted or if I don’t feel certain enough at the end, I’ll start over or repeat parts of it. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that the routine makes sense after a while. It’s more that I don’t feel allowed to stop until I’ve repeated it enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of what brought me in. I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should be straightforward, just because it doesn’t feel finished unless I do it in that specific, repeated way.\n\nDoctor: Are these repetitive checking behaviors interfering with your relationships, work, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely interfering now. Work is probably the clearest place I notice it. I’m an engineer, so there’s already a lot of checking built into the job, but this goes way beyond normal review. My project deadlines have started slipping because I’ll stay late trying to get comfortable enough to submit something. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just one more pass,” and then that turns into another pass, and another, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole evening.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s eating into the time I used to keep for myself. I used to make it to paddleboarding meetups and workouts pretty regularly, and now I’ll miss them because I’m still stuck in one of these routines or I’ve run out of time from checking things. It’s frustrating because on paper it sounds like, “Just stop and go,” but in the moment it feels like I can’t move on until I’m comfortable enough, and by then the thing I wanted to do has already passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or exact in any way when you're performing these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no — not like things have to be lined up evenly or perfectly balanced. It’s more exact in the sense that I need to feel like I checked the thing properly, in the full routine, or it doesn’t feel finished.\n\nSo with the dressing supplies, for example, it’s not that they have to look a certain way visually. It’s more like I’ll go through them, then doubt whether I really noticed each item, and then I start over. Same with door locks — I’m not making anything symmetrical, but I have to check it in the right way, like I actually confirmed it, not just glanced at it.\n\nAt work, with file dimensions or revision notes, it can feel more “exact,” I guess, because the details matter. But even then, what brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking the same things. I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops. So it’s not symmetry so much as needing the checking to feel complete enough.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these repeating thoughts and checking behaviors to be?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty distressing, honestly. I mean, I can tell myself logically that I’ve already checked the dressing supplies or the door lock or the file dimensions, but that doesn’t really make the feeling go away. It’s like there’s this pressure in my head that says, “You didn’t do it completely enough,” or “You might have missed something,” and then I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I go back through it again.\n\nWhat brings me in is really that I’m losing hours to it. It’s not just a small annoyance anymore. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then feel unsure, then go through them again in the same order. Or I’ll check a door lock and still feel like it didn’t count unless I confirmed it the right way. At work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I can get stuck doing the same pass over and over, even when part of me knows I’m not finding anything new.\n\nThe worst part is that it only feels better after I’ve repeated the routine enough times for the anxiety to drop. And even then, it’s not like I feel good — it’s more like I finally feel allowed to stop. So yeah, I’d say it’s moderately to very distressing. It’s frustrating and exhausting, because I know these things should be straightforward, but in the moment they don’t feel straightforward at all.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. Things that should be quick end up taking hours because I keep repeating the same checking actions until it feels… I don’t know, safe enough or complete enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating actions, like checking the locks or the wound area, aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s pretty much what it feels like. It’s not just checking for the sake of checking — in my head it’s tied to stopping something from going wrong, or at least making sure I didn’t leave something unsafe or unfinished.\n\nLike with the locks, I’ll think, “If I don’t check again, maybe it isn’t really locked,” even if I already checked it. With wound supplies or a dressing area, it’s more like I’m worried I missed something and then that could lead to a problem. And at work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I keep going back because I feel like if I miss one detail, it could turn into a bigger mistake.\n\nThe frustrating part is that one check doesn’t feel like enough. I’ll do it, and I can see that it’s okay, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. That’s what’s bringing me in, really — I’m losing hours to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done in a specific order or in a certain way to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, but for me it’s less like a ritual with an exact number or something, and more like I have to go through the same routine in the same kind of complete way before I can move on.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, I’ll check them, and then I’ll feel like maybe I didn’t really register one part of it, so I start again and go through the items in order. And with door locks, I can’t just glance at it casually — I feel like I have to check it in a certain way, like actually confirm it properly, or it doesn’t count in my head.\n\nAt work it happens with file dimensions and revision notes too. I’ll go back through them in a particular sequence, and if I get interrupted or if I don’t feel certain enough at the end, I’ll start over or repeat parts of it. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that the routine makes sense after a while. It’s more that I don’t feel allowed to stop until I’ve repeated it enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of what brought me in. I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should be straightforward, just because it doesn’t feel finished unless I do it in that specific, repeated way.\n\nDoctor: Are these repetitive checking behaviors interfering with your relationships, work, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely interfering now. Work is probably the clearest place I notice it. I’m an engineer, so there’s already a lot of checking built into the job, but this goes way beyond normal review. My project deadlines have started slipping because I’ll stay late trying to get comfortable enough to submit something. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just one more pass,” and then that turns into another pass, and another, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole evening.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s eating into the time I used to keep for myself. I used to make it to paddleboarding meetups and workouts pretty regularly, and now I’ll miss them because I’m still stuck in one of these routines or I’ve run out of time from checking things. It’s frustrating because on paper it sounds like, “Just stop and go,” but in the moment it feels like I can’t move on until I’m comfortable enough, and by then the thing I wanted to do has already passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or exact in any way when you're performing these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no — not like things have to be lined up evenly or perfectly balanced. It’s more exact in the sense that I need to feel like I checked the thing properly, in the full routine, or it doesn’t feel finished.\n\nSo with the dressing supplies, for example, it’s not that they have to look a certain way visually. It’s more like I’ll go through them, then doubt whether I really noticed each item, and then I start over. Same with door locks — I’m not making anything symmetrical, but I have to check it in the right way, like I actually confirmed it, not just glanced at it.\n\nAt work, with file dimensions or revision notes, it can feel more “exact,” I guess, because the details matter. But even then, what brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking the same things. I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops. So it’s not symmetry so much as needing the checking to feel complete enough.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these repeating thoughts and checking behaviors to be?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty distressing, honestly. I mean, I can tell myself logically that I’ve already checked the dressing supplies or the door lock or the file dimensions, but that doesn’t really make the feeling go away. It’s like there’s this pressure in my head that says, “You didn’t do it completely enough,” or “You might have missed something,” and then I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I go back through it again.\n\nWhat brings me in is really that I’m losing hours to it. It’s not just a small annoyance anymore. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then feel unsure, then go through them again in the same order. Or I’ll check a door lock and still feel like it didn’t count unless I confirmed it the right way. At work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I can get stuck doing the same pass over and over, even when part of me knows I’m not finding anything new.\n\nThe worst part is that it only feels better after I’ve repeated the routine enough times for the anxiety to drop. And even then, it’s not like I feel good — it’s more like I finally feel allowed to stop. So yeah, I’d say it’s moderately to very distressing. It’s frustrating and exhausting, because I know these things should be straightforward, but in the moment they don’t feel straightforward at all.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — it feels like the thought sort of cuts in, even when I’m trying to move on or focus on something totally different.\n\nLike, if I’ve cleaned the wound area and I know I did it, I can even remember doing the steps, but then this thought comes in like, “What if you missed one tiny spot?” or “What if that one thing you didn’t notice turns into an infection?” And it doesn’t feel like a normal reminder or a useful thought. It feels more like it’s forcing its way in and demanding that I pay attention to it.\n\nSame thing at work with CAD files. I’ll have reviewed something, and I’ll be trying to move on to the next task, and then suddenly I get this intrusive thought that maybe I missed one dimension or one small detail, and that could become a design mistake that hurts someone. Even if I try to reason with myself — like, “You already checked this,” or “There’s a process for catching errors” — it doesn’t really settle it. The thought keeps pushing back in.\n\nSo, yes, it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. It feels unwanted, and it interrupts whatever I’m trying to do until I go back and address it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repeating thoughts are a part of yourself or do they feel alien, like they're coming from outside of you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel like they’re coming from outside of me, no. It’s not like I hear them as a voice or feel like someone else is putting them there. They feel like my own thoughts, which is part of what makes it frustrating, I guess.\n\nBut they also don’t feel like thoughts I want to be having. It’s like they come from inside my own mind, but they’re intrusive — like they barge in and take over. I can know, for example, that I cleaned the wound area. I can picture myself doing it. Or I can know I reviewed the CAD file and went through the dimensions already. But then this thought cuts in anyway: “What if you missed one tiny thing?” And then it jumps straight to, “What if that turns into an infection?” or “What if that becomes a design mistake that hurts someone?”\n\nSo I’d say they feel like mine, but not really like me, if that makes sense. They’re not alien in the sense of coming from outside, but they feel unwanted and hard to shut off. I try to reason with them — like, “No, I already checked,” or “That’s not likely” — but it doesn’t really make them go away. They keep pressing until I respond to them somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are driven by a need to make things symmetrical or perfect in some way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. I mean, it can look like I’m trying to do things “perfectly” from the outside, because I’ll go through the same checks over and over, but it doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because something has to be evenly lined up or arranged just right.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety drops. Like with my dressing supplies, I’ll look through them, and then there’s this doubt that maybe I didn’t really confirm everything, so I go back through the same routine again. Same with door locks, or file dimensions, or revision notes at work. It’s not that they need to feel symmetrical — it’s that I don’t trust that I’m done.\n\nAnd I know that sounds kind of circular, because the checking is supposed to reassure me, but it only reassures me for a little while, or only after I’ve repeated it enough times. That’s what’s been getting bad enough that I came in — I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should take a few minutes, because until the anxiety comes down, it feels like I can’t just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: When these repeating thoughts come into your mind, are they accompanied by any specific mental images or pictures?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes, but it’s not always like a clear picture, if that makes sense. It’s more like the thought comes with a quick mental “scene” or flash of what could happen.\n\nWith the wound stuff, even after I know I cleaned the area, I’ll suddenly get this idea that I missed one tiny spot, and then my mind sort of jumps to an image of it getting infected. Not like a hallucination or anything — I know it’s in my head — but it’s vivid enough that it makes the worry feel more real. I’ll picture that I overlooked something small, and then it turns into something worse because I didn’t catch it.\n\nAnd at work, with CAD files, it’s similar. I might have reviewed the file already, but then the thought cuts in that maybe one tiny dimension or detail is wrong. And then I sort of picture the design mistake carrying forward and someone getting hurt because I didn’t catch it. It’s not always a full detailed image, sometimes it’s more like a mental impression or scenario, but it comes in very fast and it’s hard to just dismiss.\n\nThe frustrating part is that I can tell myself, “You already checked this,” but the image or scenario still kind of hangs there, like evidence against me, even though I know logically it’s not really evidence.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts mostly about the fear of making a mistake or causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the main theme of them. It’s not just a vague worry like, “Oh, I hope I did that right.” It’s more like, even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area, or I know I’ve already reviewed a CAD file, this thought cuts in really sharply that I missed one tiny thing.\n\nAnd then it doesn’t stay small. It sort of escalates immediately in my head. Like with the wound, it becomes, “What if that one thing turns into an infection?” And with work, it’s, “What if that tiny missed detail becomes a design mistake and someone gets hurt because of it?” So, yes — a lot of it is about the fear that I’ll make some mistake, even a small one, and it’ll lead to harm.\n\nThe part that bothers me is that I can’t just reason it away. I can tell myself, “You already checked,” or “You cleaned it,” or “You reviewed that file carefully,” but the thought doesn’t really respond to logic. It keeps pushing like, “But what if you didn’t?” And then I feel stuck on that possibility.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Mostly, yes. I mean, when I think about the ones that really get stuck and make me repeat things, they’re usually about preventing something bad from happening.

It’s like, even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area, the thought cuts back in that maybe I missed one tiny spot, and then my brain immediately treats that like it could turn into an infection. Or with work, after I’ve reviewed a CAD file, I’ll still get this sharp doubt that I missed one tiny detail, and then it becomes this whole fear that the mistake could carry forward and someone could get hurt because I didn’t catch it.

So it’s not really random themes, at least not the main ones. It’s usually this “what if I missed something” feeling, and then the idea that missing it could cause harm. And I can tell myself I already checked, I can even remember doing it, but it doesn’t settle the thought for long. It keeps coming back like, “But what if you didn’t really catch it?” That’s the part I can’t seem to reason my way out of.

I’ll check my dressing supplies, then check them again, and then somehow I still don’t feel finished, so I go back through the same steps. Same with the door locks — I can see that it’s locked, I can even remember locking it, but I still feel like I have to physically go back and confirm it.\n\nAt work it’s a lot of reviewing file dimensions and revision notes over and over. It’s not enough to just look once and say, “Okay, that’s done.” I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. And that can take a long time. Things that should be quick end up taking hours because I keep repeating the same checking actions until it feels… I don’t know, safe enough or complete enough.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating actions, like checking the locks or the wound area, aimed at preventing something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s pretty much what it feels like. It’s not just checking for the sake of checking — in my head it’s tied to stopping something from going wrong, or at least making sure I didn’t leave something unsafe or unfinished.\n\nLike with the locks, I’ll think, “If I don’t check again, maybe it isn’t really locked,” even if I already checked it. With wound supplies or a dressing area, it’s more like I’m worried I missed something and then that could lead to a problem. And at work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I keep going back because I feel like if I miss one detail, it could turn into a bigger mistake.\n\nThe frustrating part is that one check doesn’t feel like enough. I’ll do it, and I can see that it’s okay, but I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the same routine enough times that the anxiety finally drops. That’s what’s bringing me in, really — I’m losing hours to it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be done in a specific order or in a certain way to feel right?\n\nPatient: Yes, but for me it’s less like a ritual with an exact number or something, and more like I have to go through the same routine in the same kind of complete way before I can move on.\n\nLike with my dressing supplies, I’ll check them, and then I’ll feel like maybe I didn’t really register one part of it, so I start again and go through the items in order. And with door locks, I can’t just glance at it casually — I feel like I have to check it in a certain way, like actually confirm it properly, or it doesn’t count in my head.\n\nAt work it happens with file dimensions and revision notes too. I’ll go back through them in a particular sequence, and if I get interrupted or if I don’t feel certain enough at the end, I’ll start over or repeat parts of it. It’s really frustrating because it’s not that the routine makes sense after a while. It’s more that I don’t feel allowed to stop until I’ve repeated it enough times that the anxiety drops.\n\nThat’s honestly a big part of what brought me in. I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should be straightforward, just because it doesn’t feel finished unless I do it in that specific, repeated way.\n\nDoctor: Are these repetitive checking behaviors interfering with your relationships, work, or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely interfering now. Work is probably the clearest place I notice it. I’m an engineer, so there’s already a lot of checking built into the job, but this goes way beyond normal review. My project deadlines have started slipping because I’ll stay late trying to get comfortable enough to submit something. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just one more pass,” and then that turns into another pass, and another, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole evening.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s eating into the time I used to keep for myself. I used to make it to paddleboarding meetups and workouts pretty regularly, and now I’ll miss them because I’m still stuck in one of these routines or I’ve run out of time from checking things. It’s frustrating because on paper it sounds like, “Just stop and go,” but in the moment it feels like I can’t move on until I’m comfortable enough, and by then the thing I wanted to do has already passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or exact in any way when you're performing these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no — not like things have to be lined up evenly or perfectly balanced. It’s more exact in the sense that I need to feel like I checked the thing properly, in the full routine, or it doesn’t feel finished.\n\nSo with the dressing supplies, for example, it’s not that they have to look a certain way visually. It’s more like I’ll go through them, then doubt whether I really noticed each item, and then I start over. Same with door locks — I’m not making anything symmetrical, but I have to check it in the right way, like I actually confirmed it, not just glanced at it.\n\nAt work, with file dimensions or revision notes, it can feel more “exact,” I guess, because the details matter. But even then, what brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking the same things. I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops. So it’s not symmetry so much as needing the checking to feel complete enough.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these repeating thoughts and checking behaviors to be?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty distressing, honestly. I mean, I can tell myself logically that I’ve already checked the dressing supplies or the door lock or the file dimensions, but that doesn’t really make the feeling go away. It’s like there’s this pressure in my head that says, “You didn’t do it completely enough,” or “You might have missed something,” and then I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I go back through it again.\n\nWhat brings me in is really that I’m losing hours to it. It’s not just a small annoyance anymore. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then feel unsure, then go through them again in the same order. Or I’ll check a door lock and still feel like it didn’t count unless I confirmed it the right way. At work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I can get stuck doing the same pass over and over, even when part of me knows I’m not finding anything new.\n\nThe worst part is that it only feels better after I’ve repeated the routine enough times for the anxiety to drop. And even then, it’s not like I feel good — it’s more like I finally feel allowed to stop. So yeah, I’d say it’s moderately to very distressing. It’s frustrating and exhausting, because I know these things should be straightforward, but in the moment they don’t feel straightforward at all.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — it feels like the thought sort of cuts in, even when I’m trying to move on or focus on something totally different.\n\nLike, if I’ve cleaned the wound area and I know I did it, I can even remember doing the steps, but then this thought comes in like, “What if you missed one tiny spot?” or “What if that one thing you didn’t notice turns into an infection?” And it doesn’t feel like a normal reminder or a useful thought. It feels more like it’s forcing its way in and demanding that I pay attention to it.\n\nSame thing at work with CAD files. I’ll have reviewed something, and I’ll be trying to move on to the next task, and then suddenly I get this intrusive thought that maybe I missed one dimension or one small detail, and that could become a design mistake that hurts someone. Even if I try to reason with myself — like, “You already checked this,” or “There’s a process for catching errors” — it doesn’t really settle it. The thought keeps pushing back in.\n\nSo, yes, it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. It feels unwanted, and it interrupts whatever I’m trying to do until I go back and address it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repeating thoughts are a part of yourself or do they feel alien, like they're coming from outside of you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel like they’re coming from outside of me, no. It’s not like I hear them as a voice or feel like someone else is putting them there. They feel like my own thoughts, which is part of what makes it frustrating, I guess.\n\nBut they also don’t feel like thoughts I want to be having. It’s like they come from inside my own mind, but they’re intrusive — like they barge in and take over. I can know, for example, that I cleaned the wound area. I can picture myself doing it. Or I can know I reviewed the CAD file and went through the dimensions already. But then this thought cuts in anyway: “What if you missed one tiny thing?” And then it jumps straight to, “What if that turns into an infection?” or “What if that becomes a design mistake that hurts someone?”\n\nSo I’d say they feel like mine, but not really like me, if that makes sense. They’re not alien in the sense of coming from outside, but they feel unwanted and hard to shut off. I try to reason with them — like, “No, I already checked,” or “That’s not likely” — but it doesn’t really make them go away. They keep pressing until I respond to them somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are driven by a need to make things symmetrical or perfect in some way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. I mean, it can look like I’m trying to do things “perfectly” from the outside, because I’ll go through the same checks over and over, but it doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because something has to be evenly lined up or arranged just right.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety drops. Like with my dressing supplies, I’ll look through them, and then there’s this doubt that maybe I didn’t really confirm everything, so I go back through the same routine again. Same with door locks, or file dimensions, or revision notes at work. It’s not that they need to feel symmetrical — it’s that I don’t trust that I’m done.\n\nAnd I know that sounds kind of circular, because the checking is supposed to reassure me, but it only reassures me for a little while, or only after I’ve repeated it enough times. That’s what’s been getting bad enough that I came in — I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should take a few minutes, because until the anxiety comes down, it feels like I can’t just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: When these repeating thoughts come into your mind, are they accompanied by any specific mental images or pictures?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes, but it’s not always like a clear picture, if that makes sense. It’s more like the thought comes with a quick mental “scene” or flash of what could happen.\n\nWith the wound stuff, even after I know I cleaned the area, I’ll suddenly get this idea that I missed one tiny spot, and then my mind sort of jumps to an image of it getting infected. Not like a hallucination or anything — I know it’s in my head — but it’s vivid enough that it makes the worry feel more real. I’ll picture that I overlooked something small, and then it turns into something worse because I didn’t catch it.\n\nAnd at work, with CAD files, it’s similar. I might have reviewed the file already, but then the thought cuts in that maybe one tiny dimension or detail is wrong. And then I sort of picture the design mistake carrying forward and someone getting hurt because I didn’t catch it. It’s not always a full detailed image, sometimes it’s more like a mental impression or scenario, but it comes in very fast and it’s hard to just dismiss.\n\nThe frustrating part is that I can tell myself, “You already checked this,” but the image or scenario still kind of hangs there, like evidence against me, even though I know logically it’s not really evidence.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts mostly about the fear of making a mistake or causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the main theme of them. It’s not just a vague worry like, “Oh, I hope I did that right.” It’s more like, even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area, or I know I’ve already reviewed a CAD file, this thought cuts in really sharply that I missed one tiny thing.\n\nAnd then it doesn’t stay small. It sort of escalates immediately in my head. Like with the wound, it becomes, “What if that one thing turns into an infection?” And with work, it’s, “What if that tiny missed detail becomes a design mistake and someone gets hurt because of it?” So, yes — a lot of it is about the fear that I’ll make some mistake, even a small one, and it’ll lead to harm.\n\nThe part that bothers me is that I can’t just reason it away. I can tell myself, “You already checked,” or “You cleaned it,” or “You reviewed that file carefully,” but the thought doesn’t really respond to logic. It keeps pushing like, “But what if you didn’t?” And then I feel stuck on that possibility.\n\nDoctor: Are all of your repeating thoughts focused on preventing something bad from happening, or are there other themes as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. I mean, when I think about the ones that really get stuck and make me repeat things, they’re usually about preventing something bad from happening.\n\nIt’s like, even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area, the thought cuts back in that maybe I missed one tiny spot, and then my brain immediately treats that like it could turn into an infection. Or with work, after I’ve reviewed a CAD file, I’ll still get this sharp doubt that I missed one tiny detail, and then it becomes this whole fear that the mistake could carry forward and someone could get hurt because I didn’t catch it.\n\nSo it’s not really random themes, at least not the main ones. It’s usually this “what if I missed something” feeling, and then the idea that missing it could cause harm. And I can tell myself I already checked, I can even remember doing it, but it doesn’t settle the thought for long. It keeps coming back like, “But what if you didn’t really catch it?” That’s the part I can’t seem to reason my way out of.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It’s kind of both, but if I’m being precise, it depends on the situation. With the wound cleaning, it feels more like preventing harm to myself — like I’ll think, “What if I missed one tiny spot and now it’s going to get infected?” Even if I know I cleaned it, that thought keeps cutting back in like I didn’t do enough.

But with work, it’s more about other people. If I’ve reviewed a CAD file, I can know I looked it over carefully, but then this doubt comes in that maybe I missed one small dimension or detail, and then my mind jumps to someone getting hurt because that mistake carried forward. That one feels heavier, honestly, because it’s not just about me being anxious — it feels like I could be responsible for something happening to someone else.

So I guess the common part isn’t only “me” or “others,” it’s this fear that I missed one tiny thing and that it could turn into harm. And I can try to reason with it, like, “You already checked this,” but it doesn’t really go away just because I know that logically.

I mean, when I think about the ones that really get stuck and make me repeat things, they’re usually about preventing something bad from happening.\n\nIt’s like, even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area, the thought cuts back in that maybe I missed one tiny spot, and then my brain immediately treats that like it could turn into an infection. Or with work, after I’ve reviewed a CAD file, I’ll still get this sharp doubt that I missed one tiny detail, and then it becomes this whole fear that the mistake could carry forward and someone could get hurt because I didn’t catch it.\n\nSo it’s not really random themes, at least not the main ones. It’s usually this “what if I missed something” feeling, and then the idea that missing it could cause harm. And I can tell myself I already checked, I can even remember doing it, but it doesn’t settle the thought for long. It keeps coming back like, “But what if you didn’t really catch it?” That’s the part I can’t seem to reason my way out of.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts more about preventing harm to yourself or to others?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but if I’m being precise, it depends on the situation. With the wound cleaning, it feels more like preventing harm to myself — like I’ll think, “What if I missed one tiny spot and now it’s going to get infected?” Even if I know I cleaned it, that thought keeps cutting back in like I didn’t do enough.\n\nBut with work, it’s more about other people. That one feels heavier, honestly, because it’s not just about me being anxious — it feels like I could be responsible for something happening to someone else.\n\nSo I guess the common part isn’t only “me” or “others,” it’s this fear that I missed one tiny thing and that it could turn into harm. It keeps coming back like, “But what if you didn’t really catch it?” That’s the part I can’t seem to reason my way out of.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts more about preventing harm to yourself or to others?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but if I’m being precise, it depends on the situation. With the wound cleaning, it feels more like preventing harm to myself — like I’ll think, “What if I missed one tiny spot and now it’s going to get infected?” Even if I know I cleaned it, that thought keeps cutting back in like I didn’t do enough.\n\nBut with work, it’s more about other people. If I’ve reviewed a CAD file, I can know I looked it over carefully, but then this doubt comes in that maybe I missed one small dimension or detail, and then my mind jumps to someone getting hurt because that mistake carried forward. That one feels heavier, honestly, because it’s not just about me being anxious — it feels like I could be responsible for something happening to someone else.\n\nSo I guess the common part isn’t only “me” or “others,” it’s this fear that I missed one tiny thing and that it could turn into harm. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
More often, I think it’s about someone else getting hurt, especially when it’s connected to work. Like if I’ve reviewed a CAD file, I can know I looked through it, but then this thought cuts in that maybe I missed one tiny thing — one dimension, one small detail — and then my mind runs with it to, “What if that turns into a design mistake and somebody gets hurt because I didn’t catch it?”

But it’s not only other people. With something like cleaning a wound, it’s more about something bad happening to me — like I missed one tiny spot and then it becomes an infection. So it shifts depending on what I’m doing.

The common part is really that feeling of, “What if I missed something?” And once that thought gets in, I can’t really just talk myself out of it, even if I know I already cleaned it or already reviewed the file. It keeps coming back like there’s still some small chance I overlooked something, and that chance feels hard to leave alone.

I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just one more pass,” and then that turns into another pass, and another, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole evening.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s eating into the time I used to keep for myself. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Okay, just one more pass,” and then that turns into another pass, and another, and suddenly I’ve lost the whole evening.\n\nAnd outside of work, it’s eating into the time I used to keep for myself. It’s frustrating because on paper it sounds like, “Just stop and go,” but in the moment it feels like I can’t move on until I’m comfortable enough, and by then the thing I wanted to do has already passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things need to be symmetrical or exact in any way when you're performing these checking routines?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no — not like things have to be lined up evenly or perfectly balanced. It’s more exact in the sense that I need to feel like I checked the thing properly, in the full routine, or it doesn’t feel finished.\n\nSo with the dressing supplies, for example, it’s not that they have to look a certain way visually. It’s more like I’ll go through them, then doubt whether I really noticed each item, and then I start over. Same with door locks — I’m not making anything symmetrical, but I have to check it in the right way, like I actually confirmed it, not just glanced at it.\n\nAt work, with file dimensions or revision notes, it can feel more “exact,” I guess, because the details matter. But even then, what brings me in is that I’m losing hours rechecking the same things. I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve gone through the routine enough times that the anxiety drops. So it’s not symmetry so much as needing the checking to feel complete enough.\n\nDoctor: How distressing do you find these repeating thoughts and checking behaviors to be?\n\nPatient: It’s pretty distressing, honestly. I mean, I can tell myself logically that I’ve already checked the dressing supplies or the door lock or the file dimensions, but that doesn’t really make the feeling go away. It’s like there’s this pressure in my head that says, “You didn’t do it completely enough,” or “You might have missed something,” and then I feel like I’m not allowed to move on until I go back through it again.\n\nWhat brings me in is really that I’m losing hours to it. It’s not just a small annoyance anymore. I’ll check my dressing supplies, then feel unsure, then go through them again in the same order. Or I’ll check a door lock and still feel like it didn’t count unless I confirmed it the right way. At work, with file dimensions or revision notes, I can get stuck doing the same pass over and over, even when part of me knows I’m not finding anything new.\n\nThe worst part is that it only feels better after I’ve repeated the routine enough times for the anxiety to drop. And even then, it’s not like I feel good — it’s more like I finally feel allowed to stop. So yeah, I’d say it’s moderately to very distressing. It’s frustrating and exhausting, because I know these things should be straightforward, but in the moment they don’t feel straightforward at all.\n\nDoctor: Do these repeating thoughts feel like they're forcing their way into your mind, even when you're trying to think about something else?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a good way to put it — it feels like the thought sort of cuts in, even when I’m trying to move on or focus on something totally different.\n\nLike, if I’ve cleaned the wound area and I know I did it, I can even remember doing the steps, but then this thought comes in like, “What if you missed one tiny spot?” or “What if that one thing you didn’t notice turns into an infection?” And it doesn’t feel like a normal reminder or a useful thought. It feels more like it’s forcing its way in and demanding that I pay attention to it.\n\nSame thing at work with CAD files. I’ll have reviewed something, and I’ll be trying to move on to the next task, and then suddenly I get this intrusive thought that maybe I missed one dimension or one small detail, and that could become a design mistake that hurts someone. Even if I try to reason with myself — like, “You already checked this,” or “There’s a process for catching errors” — it doesn’t really settle it. The thought keeps pushing back in.\n\nSo, yes, it’s not like I’m choosing to think about it. It feels unwanted, and it interrupts whatever I’m trying to do until I go back and address it somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these repeating thoughts are a part of yourself or do they feel alien, like they're coming from outside of you?\n\nPatient: They don’t feel like they’re coming from outside of me, no. It’s not like I hear them as a voice or feel like someone else is putting them there. They feel like my own thoughts, which is part of what makes it frustrating, I guess.\n\nBut they also don’t feel like thoughts I want to be having. It’s like they come from inside my own mind, but they’re intrusive — like they barge in and take over. I can know, for example, that I cleaned the wound area. I can picture myself doing it. Or I can know I reviewed the CAD file and went through the dimensions already. But then this thought cuts in anyway: “What if you missed one tiny thing?” And then it jumps straight to, “What if that turns into an infection?” or “What if that becomes a design mistake that hurts someone?”\n\nSo I’d say they feel like mine, but not really like me, if that makes sense. They’re not alien in the sense of coming from outside, but they feel unwanted and hard to shut off. I try to reason with them — like, “No, I already checked,” or “That’s not likely” — but it doesn’t really make them go away. They keep pressing until I respond to them somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your checking behaviors are driven by a need to make things symmetrical or perfect in some way?\n\nPatient: Not really symmetrical, no. I mean, it can look like I’m trying to do things “perfectly” from the outside, because I’ll go through the same checks over and over, but it doesn’t feel like I’m doing it because something has to be evenly lined up or arranged just right.\n\nIt’s more that I don’t feel allowed to move on until I’ve checked enough times that the anxiety drops. Like with my dressing supplies, I’ll look through them, and then there’s this doubt that maybe I didn’t really confirm everything, so I go back through the same routine again. Same with door locks, or file dimensions, or revision notes at work. It’s not that they need to feel symmetrical — it’s that I don’t trust that I’m done.\n\nAnd I know that sounds kind of circular, because the checking is supposed to reassure me, but it only reassures me for a little while, or only after I’ve repeated it enough times. That’s what’s been getting bad enough that I came in — I’m losing hours to rechecking things that should take a few minutes, because until the anxiety comes down, it feels like I can’t just leave it alone.\n\nDoctor: When these repeating thoughts come into your mind, are they accompanied by any specific mental images or pictures?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes, but it’s not always like a clear picture, if that makes sense. It’s more like the thought comes with a quick mental “scene” or flash of what could happen.\n\nWith the wound stuff, even after I know I cleaned the area, I’ll suddenly get this idea that I missed one tiny spot, and then my mind sort of jumps to an image of it getting infected. Not like a hallucination or anything — I know it’s in my head — but it’s vivid enough that it makes the worry feel more real. I’ll picture that I overlooked something small, and then it turns into something worse because I didn’t catch it.\n\nAnd at work, with CAD files, it’s similar. I might have reviewed the file already, but then the thought cuts in that maybe one tiny dimension or detail is wrong. And then I sort of picture the design mistake carrying forward and someone getting hurt because I didn’t catch it. It’s not always a full detailed image, sometimes it’s more like a mental impression or scenario, but it comes in very fast and it’s hard to just dismiss.\n\nThe frustrating part is that I can tell myself, “You already checked this,” but the image or scenario still kind of hangs there, like evidence against me, even though I know logically it’s not really evidence.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts mostly about the fear of making a mistake or causing harm to yourself or others?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s probably the main theme of them. It’s not just a vague worry like, “Oh, I hope I did that right.” It’s more like, even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area, or I know I’ve already reviewed a CAD file, this thought cuts in really sharply that I missed one tiny thing.\n\nAnd then it doesn’t stay small. It sort of escalates immediately in my head. Like with the wound, it becomes, “What if that one thing turns into an infection?” And with work, it’s, “What if that tiny missed detail becomes a design mistake and someone gets hurt because of it?” So, yes — a lot of it is about the fear that I’ll make some mistake, even a small one, and it’ll lead to harm.\n\nThe part that bothers me is that I can’t just reason it away. I can tell myself, “You already checked,” or “You cleaned it,” or “You reviewed that file carefully,” but the thought doesn’t really respond to logic. It keeps pushing like, “But what if you didn’t?” And then I feel stuck on that possibility.\n\nDoctor: Are all of your repeating thoughts focused on preventing something bad from happening, or are there other themes as well?\n\nPatient: Mostly, yes. I mean, when I think about the ones that really get stuck and make me repeat things, they’re usually about preventing something bad from happening.\n\nIt’s like, even after I know I’ve cleaned the wound area, the thought cuts back in that maybe I missed one tiny spot, and then my brain immediately treats that like it could turn into an infection. Or with work, after I’ve reviewed a CAD file, I’ll still get this sharp doubt that I missed one tiny detail, and then it becomes this whole fear that the mistake could carry forward and someone could get hurt because I didn’t catch it.\n\nSo it’s not really random themes, at least not the main ones. It’s usually this “what if I missed something” feeling, and then the idea that missing it could cause harm. And I can tell myself I already checked, I can even remember doing it, but it doesn’t settle the thought for long. It keeps coming back like, “But what if you didn’t really catch it?” That’s the part I can’t seem to reason my way out of.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts more about preventing harm to yourself or to others?\n\nPatient: It’s kind of both, but if I’m being precise, it depends on the situation. With the wound cleaning, it feels more like preventing harm to myself — like I’ll think, “What if I missed one tiny spot and now it’s going to get infected?” Even if I know I cleaned it, that thought keeps cutting back in like I didn’t do enough.\n\nBut with work, it’s more about other people. If I’ve reviewed a CAD file, I can know I looked it over carefully, but then this doubt comes in that maybe I missed one small dimension or detail, and then my mind jumps to someone getting hurt because that mistake carried forward. That one feels heavier, honestly, because it’s not just about me being anxious — it feels like I could be responsible for something happening to someone else.\n\nSo I guess the common part isn’t only “me” or “others,” it’s this fear that I missed one tiny thing and that it could turn into harm. And I can try to reason with it, like, “You already checked this,” but it doesn’t really go away just because I know that logically.\n\nDoctor: Are your repeating thoughts more often about something bad happening to you or to someone else?\n\nPatient: More often, I think it’s about someone else getting hurt, especially when it’s connected to work. Like if I’ve reviewed a CAD file, I can know I looked through it, but then this thought cuts in that maybe I missed one tiny thing — one dimension, one small detail — and then my mind runs with it to, “What if that turns into a design mistake and somebody gets hurt because I didn’t catch it?”\n\nBut it’s not only other people. With something like cleaning a wound, it’s more about something bad happening to me — like I missed one tiny spot and then it becomes an infection. So it shifts depending on what I’m doing.\n\nThe common part is really that feeling of, “What if I missed something?” And once that thought gets in, I can’t really just talk myself out of it, even if I know I already cleaned it or already reviewed the file. It keeps coming back like there’s still some small chance I overlooked something, and that chance feels hard to leave alone.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

More often it’s about preventing harm to others, I think, especially when it’s tied to work. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.0, F42.1, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. And I know that sounds excessive, but in the moment the anxiety is really uncomfortable, and it only drops after I’ve checked the same things repeatedly. So I end up spending hours on things that should take minutes, and it’s starting to get hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts that keep repeating in your mind, even when you try to stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s actually a big part of it. It’s not just that I decide to check something — it’s like the thought cuts in on its own and keeps coming back.\n\nLike with cleaning a wound area, even after I’ve already done it and I can look at it and know, logically, that I cleaned it, I’ll get this thought that maybe I missed one tiny spot. It feels like I’m pushed into doing the action, not just thinking about it. The main one is rechecking.
